People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Just everyday stuff, like door locks or my dressing supplies... and work things, too, like file dimensions and revision notes. \n\nI just... I don't feel allowed to just move on. I end up going through the same routine over and over, enough times until... I don't know, until the anxiety finally drops. So... yeah. That's what brings me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I guess so. It's just... well, even when I know I've already cleaned a wound area, or... reviewed a CAD file for work... this thought just keeps cutting in. \n\nLike I missed one tiny thing. And... that it's going to turn into an infection, or a design mistake that ends up hurting somebody. I try to just reason it away, but... I can't really do that. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or routines in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah... um, I guess. Like I was saying before, it's... it's a routine. I just have to go through it a certain way. \n\nIf I don't do it right, or if I try to rush it... I just don't feel allowed to move on. The anxiety won't drop unless I do it exactly like that. So... yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing images or pictures that come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I don't... I don't think I see pictures or anything like that. It's mostly just... the thoughts I mentioned before.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these checks or routines?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I guess so. I just don't want those bad things to happen. But... it's taking up a lot of my day. \n\nI end up losing hours just... rechecking things. The door locks, my dressing supplies... and at work, it's the file dimensions, or revision notes. I just... I don't feel allowed to move on until I've gone through the routine enough times. Just... until the anxiety drops.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety or to actually prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess both. Like I was just saying, I... I just can't move on until the anxiety drops. If I don't repeat the routine with the locks or my files enough times, that feeling just stays there. So... I guess I'm mostly just doing it to make that stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform physical actions, like touching or tapping, in a certain way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not tapping or... touching things in a special way. Just... like I mentioned before, it's the rechecking. Physically going through the same routine with the locks, or my supplies... I just have to do it enough times. That's... that's really it.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive thoughts and checking behaviors affecting your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I guess they are. At work, I've... I've been missing some deadlines lately. I just end up staying late, trying to... get comfortable enough with the files to actually submit them. And, um... outside of work... I don't really go to my paddleboarding meetups anymore. Or work out. By the time I get through the routines, I just... I don't have the time left for that stuff.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about something bad happening to yourself or to others?\n\nPatient: Um... mostly others, I guess. Like... with the CAD files at work, I... I know I reviewed them. But this thought just cuts in that I missed something tiny, and... someone's going to get hurt because of a design mistake. Or... like if I have a small cut, I know I cleaned it, but I just keep thinking I missed a spot and it's going to get infected. I... I can't just reason it away. It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right, in addition to being checked repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I don't... I don't really care if things are lined up or symmetrical. It's just... the checking. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts and checking behaviors focused on preventing specific types of harm, like injury or illness?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I guess so. Just... like what I mentioned before. People getting hurt from the designs, or... getting sick from a cut. I don't really know what else to call it. I just... don't want those things to happen. That's pretty much it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are unwanted and coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah... they are. I don't want them there. Like I was saying before... even after I know I’ve cleaned a wound, or I know I reviewed a CAD file, the thought just cuts in anyway. It just tells me I missed one tiny thing and it’s going to turn into an infection, or... a design mistake that hurts someone. I don't invite it. I try to reason it away, but... I can't. It just forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are a part of you or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't know. They're my thoughts, I guess. I mean... I'm the one having them. They don't feel like... someone else putting them there, if that's what you mean. Just... my brain getting stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are necessary to prevent harm from occurring to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I guess that's the idea. It's just... that's kind of what brought me in. I'm losing hours just... rechecking things. Like the file dimensions at work, revision notes... door locks, my dressing supplies. I just... I don't feel allowed to move on to the next thing until I've gone through the same routine enough times. Just... until the anxiety drops. So... yeah. I guess I feel like I have to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like I was saying before, it's... it's a routine. I just have to go through it a certain way. \n\nIf I don't do it right, or if I try to rush it... I just don't feel allowed to move on. The anxiety won't drop unless I do it exactly like that. So... yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any disturbing images or pictures that come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I don't... Just... until the anxiety drops.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety or to actually prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess both. Like I was just saying, I... I just can't move on until the anxiety drops. If I don't repeat the routine with the locks or my files enough times, that feeling just stays there. So... I guess I'm mostly just doing it to make that stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform physical actions, like touching or tapping, in a certain way to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not tapping or... touching things in a special way. I've been missing some deadlines lately. I just end up staying late, trying to... get comfortable enough with the files to actually submit them. And, um... outside of work... It just stays there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or just right, in addition to being checked repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I don't... I don't really care if things are lined up or symmetrical. It's just... the checking. It just forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are a part of you or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't know. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.
